\displaystyle\longrightarrow{\int\!\!\!\!\int\!\!\!\!\int}_{\Im_{1},\Im_{2},\Im_{3}}U_{a}(x,t)U_{b}(y,t)U_{z}(z,t)dx^{a}dy^{b}dz^{k}

(8.41)

8.3.
Hopf-like functional integrals for turbulent flows

Hopf constructed a functional integral to describe a turbulent flow
[88-91]
. By applying the Navier-Stokes equation to the moment-generating functional for
velocity, a nonlinear differential equation describing a single flow realization is transformed to a linear functional integro-differential equation governing
an ensemble of flows. However, this theory remains underdeveloped mathematically and is hampered by a lack of solutions or methods to obtain solutions.
[See REF and references theirin].

To see how the Hopf functional integral arises, consider the Burgers equation in 1D, which is the NS equation with the pressure term dropped so that

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}U(x,t)+U({x},t)\frac{\partial}{\partial x}U(x,t)=\nu\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}},~~x~\in\mathbb{R},t>0

(8.42)

As mentioned, this equation has been extensively studied. The Hopf characteristic functional for the velocity is then

\displaystyle{\Phi}[{{\psi}}(x)]=\left\langle\exp\left(i\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}{f}(x)U(x)dx\right)\right\rangle

(8.43)

where
{\psi}
is a ’test function’ and
\langle\bullet\rangle
is a generic ’ensemble average’. Then
{\Phi}[{f}(x)]
satisfies the
Hopf functional differential equation

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}{\Phi}[{f}(x)]={\mathcal{L}}{\Phi}[{f}(x)]

\displaystyle=\frac{i}{2}\int{\psi}(x)\frac{\partial}{\partial x}\frac{\delta^{2}\Phi}{\delta{\psi}(x)^{2}}dx+\nu\int{\psi}(x)\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}\frac{\delta\Phi}{\delta{\psi}(x)}dx

(8.44)

where
\delta/\delta{\psi}(x)
is a functional derivative. It is required that
{\Phi}[{f}(x)]=1
at

{f}(x)=0
and that positive-definite condition is imposed

\displaystyle\sum_{\ell=1}^{N}\sum_{k=1}^{N}{\Phi}[{f}_{k}(x)-{{\psi}}_{\ell}(x)]\mathrm{C}_{k}\mathrm{C}_{p}=\left\langle\left|\sum_{k=1}^{N}{\mathrm{C}}_{k}\exp\left(i\int U(x,t)f(x)dx\right)\right|^{2}\right\rangle

(8.45)

for constants
\mathrm{C}_{k},\mathrm{C}_{l}
and
N=1,2,3...
. Although this FDE is linear, there is no known general method to find solutions. However,
if the nonlinear term in the Burgers equation is very small or set to zero then the Burgers’ equations reduces to the form of the heat equation

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}U(x,t)=\nu\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}U(x,t)

(8.46)

The HFDE then reduces to

\displaystyle\frac{\partial}{\partial t}{\Phi}[{f}(x)]=\nu\int{f}(x)\frac{\partial^{2}}{\partial x^{2}}\frac{\delta{\Phi}}{\delta{f}(x)}dx

(8.47)

which has a solution in terms of the heat kernel

\displaystyle{\Phi}[{f}(x),t]=\Phi_{o}\left[\frac{1}{\sqrt{4\pi\nu t}}\int\exp\left(-\frac{|x-y|^{2}}{4\pi\nu}\right){{\psi}}(y)dy\right]

(8.48)

This is the Hopf-Titt solution
[91]
. It is also well known that a Cole-Hopf transform takes the Burgers equation to the heat equation form. [Evans]
By analogy, one can tentatively propose a Hopf-like functional integral for the random field
{\mathscr{U}}_{a}(x,t)
.

Proposition 8.13
.

Let
\bm{\mathfrak{D}}\subset\mathbb{R}^{3}
and let
U_{a}(x,t)
be a smooth underlying flow evolving by the NS equations. If
\Im\in\bm{\mathfrak{D}}
is
a curve or knot then the circulation is
\mathbf{C}[U]=\int_{\Im}U_{a}(x,t)dx^{a}
. Define a Hopf functional or ”Wilson line” as

\displaystyle\mathbf{H}[U]=\exp\left(i\int_{\Im}~{f}(x,t)U_{a}(x,t)dx^{a}\right)=\exp(\mathbf{C}[U])

(8.49)

Now given the turbulent flow or random field
{\mathscr{U}}_{a}(x,t)
then define a stochastic Hopf-like integral as

\displaystyle{\mathscr{W}}[{{\mathscr{U}}}_{a}]=\exp(\left(i{\int}_{\Im}\left({f}(x,t)U_{a}(x,t)+{\bm{\bm{\alpha}}}{f}(x,t)U_{a}(x,t){{\psi}}\big(\mathbf{Re}(\bm{\mathfrak{D}},t);{\mathbb{I}}\big){{\mathscr{B}}}(x)dx^{a}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(i{\int}_{\Im}\left({f}(x,t)U_{a}(x,t)dx^{a}+{\bm{\bm{\alpha}}}{f}(x,t){\int}_{\Im}U_{a}(x,t){{\psi}}\big(\mathbf{Re}(\bm{\mathfrak{D}},t);{\mathbb{I}}\big){{\mathscr{B}}}(x)dx^{a}\right)\right)

\displaystyle=\exp\left(i{\int}_{\Im}{f}(x,t)U_{a}(x,t)dx^{a}\right)\exp(\left({i\bm{\bm{\alpha}}}{f}(x,t){\int}_{\Im}U_{a}(x,t){{\psi}}\big(\mathbf{Re}(\bm{\mathfrak{D}},t);{\mathbb{I}}\big){{\mathscr{B}}}(x)dx^{a}\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbf{H}[U]\exp\left({i\bm{\bm{\alpha}}}{{f}}(x,t){\int}_{\Im}U_{a}(x,t){{\psi}}\big(\mathbf{Re}(\bm{\mathfrak{D}},t);{\mathbb{I}}\big){{\mathscr{B}}}(x)dx^{a}\right)\equiv

\displaystyle=\mathbf{H}[U]\exp\left({i\bm{\bm{\alpha}}}{f}(x,t){\int}_{\Im}{{\psi}}\big(\mathbf{Re}(\bm{\mathfrak{D}},t);{\mathbb{I}}\big){{\mathscr{B}}}(x)U_{a}(x,t)dx^{a}\right)

(8.50)

The stochastic expectation is then

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathsf{H}}}[{{\mathscr{U}}}_{a};\Im]={\bm{\mathbb{I\!E}}}\bigg\langle{\mathscr{W}}[{{\mathscr{U}}}_{a}]\bigg\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathbf{H}[U;\Im]\times{\bm{\mathbb{I\!E}}}\left\langle\exp\left({i\bm{\bm{\alpha}}}{{{f}}}(x,t){\int}_{\Im}{{\psi}}\big(\mathbf{Re}(\bm{\mathfrak{D}},t);{\mathbb{I}}\big){{\mathscr{B}}}(x)U_{a}(x,t)dx^{a}\right)\right\rangle

(8.51)

For a close curve or knot
\mathfrak{K}

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathsf{H}}}[{{\mathscr{U}}}_{a};\mathfrak{K}]={\bm{\mathbb{I\!E}}}\bigg\langle{\mathscr{W}}[{{\mathscr{U}}}_{a}]\bigg\rangle

\displaystyle=\mathbf{H}[U;\mathfrak{K}]\times{\bm{\mathbb{I\!E}}}\left\langle\exp\left({i\bm{\bm{\alpha}}}{{{f}}}(x,t){\oint}_{\Im}{{\psi}}\big(\mathbf{Re}(\bm{\mathfrak{D}},t);{\mathbb{I}}\big){{\mathscr{B}}}(x)U_{a}(x,t)dx^{a}\right)\right\rangle

(8.52)

Corollary 8.14
.

When the average Reynolds number within
\bm{\mathfrak{D}}
is below the critical value then the turbulence subsides and
\big\{{{\psi}}\big(\mathbf{Re}(\bm{\mathfrak{D}},t);{\mathbb{I}}\big)\big\}=0
. Then the Hopf-like integral reduces to

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathsf{H}}}[{{\mathscr{U}}}_{a};\Im]=\mathbf{H}[U;\Im]

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathsf{H}}}[{{\mathscr{U}}}_{a};\mathcal{O}]=\mathbf{H}[U;\mathcal{O}]

It should be possible to expand the stochastic integral out as a ’cluster expansion’ or Van Kampen-type expansion of cumulants (REF).
This is involved however, and may be the subject of a future article.

Appendix A
Proof of Lemma

Proof.

To prove (2.53)

\displaystyle\bm{\mathbb{I\!E}}\bigg\langle{\mathscr{G}}(x){{\boxtimes}}~{\mathscr{G}}(y)\bigg\rangle={\int}_{\mathbf{K}^{3}}d^{3}k\bm{\Phi}(k)\exp(ik_{a}(x-y)^{a})

\displaystyle=\bm{\bm{\alpha}}{\int}_{\mathbf{K}^{3}}d^{3}k\exp(ik_{a}(x-y)^{a})=\bm{\bm{\alpha}}\delta^{3}(x-y)

(A.1)

To prove (2.54)

\displaystyle\bm{\mathbb{I\!E}}\bigg\langle{\mathscr{G}}(x){{\boxtimes}}~{\mathscr{G}}(y)\bigg\rangle=\int_{\mathbf{K}^{3}}d^{3}\mathbf{k}\bm{\Phi}(\mathbf{k})\exp(ik_{a}(x-y)^{a})

\displaystyle=\bm{\bm{\alpha}}\int_{\mathbf{K}^{3}}d^{3}\mathbf{k}\frac{\exp(-\tfrac{1}{4}\ell^{2}\mathbf{k}^{2})}{\mathbf{k}^{2}}\exp(ik_{a}(x-y)^{a})

\displaystyle=\bm{\bm{\alpha}}\int_{\mathbf{K}^{3}}d^{3}\mathbf{k}\frac{\exp(-\tfrac{1}{4}\ell^{2}\mathbf{k}^{2}+ik_{a}(x-y)^{a})}{\mathbf{k}^{2}}

\displaystyle\equiv\bm{\bm{\alpha}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int d\mathbf{k}\mathbf{k}^{2}d\omega\frac{\exp\big(-\frac{1}{4}\ell^{2}\mathbf{k}^{2}+ik_{a}(x-y)^{a}\big)}{\mathbf{k}^{2}}

\displaystyle\equiv\bm{\bm{\alpha}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\int d\mathbf{k}d\omega\exp\big(-\tfrac{1}{4}\ell^{2}\mathbf{k}^{2}+ik_{a}(x-y)^{a}\big)

(A.2)

The angular integral
\int d\Omega
just contributes a constant factor which an be absorbed into
\bm{\bm{\alpha}}
giving

\displaystyle\bm{\mathbb{I\!E}}\bigg\langle{\mathscr{G}}(x){{\boxtimes}}~{\mathscr{G}}(y)\bigg\rangle=\equiv\overline{\bm{\bm{\alpha}}}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\mathbf{k}\exp\big(-\tfrac{1}{4}\ell^{2}k_{a}^{2}+ik_{a}(x-y)^{a}\big)

The square is then completed on the integrand

\displaystyle-\tfrac{1}{4}\ell^{2}k_{a}^{2}-ik_{a}(x-y)^{a}=-ak_{a}^{2}+bk_{a}=-a\left(k_{a}-\frac{b}{2a}\right)^{2}+\frac{b}{4a}

(A.4)

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathbb{I\!E}}}\bigg\langle{\mathscr{G}}(x){{\boxtimes}}~{\mathscr{G}}(y)\bigg\rangle=\equiv\overline{\bm{\bm{\alpha}}}{\int}_{-\infty}^{\infty}dk\exp\big(-\tfrac{1}{4}\ell^{2}k_{a}^{2}+ik_{a}(x-y)^{a}\big)

\displaystyle=\overline{\bm{\bm{\alpha}}}{\int}_{-\infty}^{\infty}dk\exp\left(k_{a}-\frac{(x-y)_{a}}{\ell^{2}}\right)^{2}\exp\left(-\frac{(x-y)_{a}(x-y)^{a})}{\ell^{2}}\right)

\displaystyle\equiv\overline{\bm{\bm{\alpha}}}\exp\left(-\frac{(x-y)_{a}(x-y)^{a})}{\ell^{2}}\right)\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dk\exp\left(k_{a}-\frac{(x-y)_{a}}{\ell^{2}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle\equiv\overline{\bm{\bm{\alpha}}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{\ell^{2}}\right)\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dk\exp\left(k_{a}-\frac{(x-y)_{a}}{\ell^{2}}\right)^{2}

(A.5)

Setting
\overline{k_{a}}=k_{a}-\frac{(x-y)_{a}}{\ell^{2}}
then
d\overline{k}=dk
so that

\displaystyle\bm{\mathbb{I\!E}}\bigg\langle{\mathscr{G}}(x){{\boxtimes}}~{\mathscr{G}}(y)\bigg\rangle=\overline{\mathfrak{K}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{\ell^{2}}\right)\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}dk\exp\left(k_{a}-\frac{(x-y)_{a}}{\ell^{2}}\right)^{2}

\displaystyle=\overline{\mathfrak{K}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{\ell^{2}}\right)\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d\overline{k}\exp(\overline{k}^{2})

(A.6)

The basic Gaussian integral and this constant factor can again be absorbed into
\overline{\bm{\bm{\alpha}}}
so that

\displaystyle{\mathbb{I\!E}}\bigg\langle{\mathscr{G}}(x){{\boxtimes}}~{\mathscr{G}}(y)\bigg\rangle={\mathbb{I\!E}}\bigg\langle{\mathscr{G}}(x){{\boxtimes}}~{\mathscr{G}}(y)=\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{\ell^{2}}\right)

(A.7)

∎

Appendix B
Derivatives of BF fields

To derive the spatial derivatives
\nabla_{a}{{\mathscr{B}}}(x,t)),\nabla_{a}\nabla_{b}-+-(x,t))
one first requires the following preliminary lemmas.

Lemma 2.1
.

Let
(x,y)\in\mathbb{Q}\in\bm{\mathbb{R}}^{3}
then

\displaystyle\nabla_{a}^{(x)}\|x-y\|^{2}\equiv\frac{\partial}{\partial x_{a}}\|x-y\|^{2}=6(x-y)_{a}

\displaystyle\nabla_{b}^{(y)}\|x-y\|^{2}\equiv\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{b}}\|x-y\|^{2}=-6(x-y)_{b}=6(y-x)_{b}

(B.1)

and the second derivative is

\displaystyle\nabla_{a}^{x}\nabla_{b}^{(y)}\|x-y\|=18\delta_{ij}

(B.2)

Proof.

The derivative is quite obvious but can still be carried out in detail

\displaystyle\nabla_{a}^{(x)}\|x-y\|^{2}=\nabla_{a}^{(x)}(x-y)_{a}(x-y)^{a}=\nabla_{a}^{(x)}(x-y)_{a}(x-y)^{a}

\displaystyle=\nabla_{a}^{(x)}(x_{a}-y_{a})(x^{a}-y^{a})=\nabla_{a}^{(x)}(x_{a}x^{a}-x_{a}y^{a}-x^{a}y_{a}-y_{a}y^{a})

\displaystyle=\nabla_{a}^{(x)}(x^{a}x^{a}\delta_{ii}-x^{a}y^{a}\delta_{ii}-x^{a}y^{a}\delta_{ii}-y^{a}y^{a}\delta_{ii})

\displaystyle=2x^{a}\nabla_{a}^{(x)}x^{a}\delta_{ii}-2y^{a}\nabla_{a}x^{a}\delta_{ii}-2y^{a}\nabla^{a}x^{a}\delta_{ii}=2\nabla_{a}^{(x)}(x^{a}-y^{a})\delta_{ii}=6(x-y)_{a}

(B.3)

since
divx=\nabla_{a}^{(x)}x^{a}=3
in
\mathbf{R}^{3}
.
∎

Similarly,
\nabla_{b}^{(y)}\|x-y\|^{2}\equiv\frac{\partial}{\partial y_{b}}\|x-y\|^{2}=-6(x-y)_{b}=6(y-x)_{b}
. Then (B2) follows immediately.

Lemma 2.2
.

Given the Gaussian kernel
{\mathsf{\Sigma}}(x,y;\lambda)=\mathsf{C}\exp(-\|x-y\|\lambda^{-2})
, the first derivatives are

\displaystyle{\mathsf{\Phi}}_{a}^{(x)}(x,y;\lambda)=\nabla_{a}^{(x)}{\mathsf{\Sigma}}(x,y;\lambda)=-\frac{6(x-y)_{a}}{\lambda^{2}}{{\mathsf{\Sigma}}}(x,y;\lambda)

(B.4)

\displaystyle{\mathsf{\Phi}}_{a}^{(y)}(x,y;\lambda)=\nabla_{b}^{(y)}{\mathsf{\Sigma}}(x,y;\lambda)=+\frac{6(x-y)_{b}}{\lambda^{2}}{\mathsf{\Sigma}}(x,y;\lambda)

(B.5)

and the second derivative is

\displaystyle{\mathsf{\Phi}}_{ij}(x,t;\lambda)=\nabla_{a}^{(y)}\nabla_{b}^{(x)}{{\psi}}(x,y;\lambda)=\frac{18\delta_{ij}}{\lambda^{2}}{\mathsf{\Sigma}}(x,y;\ell)+\frac{6(x-y)_{a}}{\lambda^{2}}\nabla_{a}^{(x)}{\mathsf{\Phi}}(x,y;\lambda)

\displaystyle=\frac{18\delta_{ij}}{\lambda^{2}}{\mathsf{\Phi}}(x,y;\ell)-\frac{36(x-y)_{a}(x-y)_{b}}{\lambda^{2}}{\mathsf{\Phi}}(x,y;\lambda)

(B.6)

Then also

\displaystyle\lim_{y\rightarrow x}\nabla_{a}^{x}{\mathsf{\Sigma}}(x,y;\lambda)=-\lim_{y\rightarrow x}\frac{6(x-y)_{a}}{\lambda^{2}}{\mathsf{\Sigma}}(x,y;\lambda)=0

(B.7)

Proof.

Taking the log of both sides of the Gaussian kernel then

\displaystyle\log{\mathsf{\Sigma}}(x,y;\lambda)=\log\alpha-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{\lambda^{2}}

(B.8)

so that

\displaystyle\frac{\nabla_{a}^{(x)}{\mathsf{\Sigma}}(x,y;\lambda)}{{\mathsf{\Sigma}}(x,y;\lambda)}=-\frac{\nabla_{a}^{(x)}\|x-y\|}{\lambda^{2}}=-\frac{6(x-y)_{a}}{\lambda^{2}}

(B.9)

and the derivative is

\displaystyle\nabla_{a}^{(x)}{\mathsf{\Sigma}}(x,y;\lambda)=-\frac{\nabla_{a}^{(x)}\|x-y\|}{\lambda^{2}}=-\frac{6(x-y)_{a}}{\lambda^{2}}{\mathsf{\Sigma}}(x,y;\lambda)

(B.10)

The 2nd derivative is

\displaystyle\nabla_{a}^{(x)}\nabla_{b}^{(y)}{\mathsf{\Sigma}}(x,;\lambda)=\frac{36(x-y)_{a}(x-y)_{b}}{\lambda^{4}}{\mathsf{\Sigma}}(x,y;\lambda)+\frac{18\delta_{ij}}{\lambda^{2}}{\mathsf{\Sigma}}(x,y;\lambda)

(B.11)

In the limit as
y\rightarrow x

\displaystyle\lim_{y\uparrow x}\nabla_{a}^{(x)}{\mathsf{\Sigma}}(x,y;\lambda)=-\lim_{y\uparrow x}\frac{6(x-y)_{a}}{\lambda^{2}}{\mathsf{\Sigma}}(x,y;\lambda)=0

(B.12)

\displaystyle\lim_{y\uparrow x}\nabla_{a}^{(x)}\nabla_{b}^{(y)}{\mathsf{\Sigma}}(x,y;\lambda)=\lim_{y\uparrow x}\left(\frac{36(x-y)_{a}(x-y)_{b}}{\lambda^{4}}{\mathsf{\Sigma}}(x,y;\lambda)+\frac{18\delta_{ij}}{\lambda^{2}}{\mathsf{\Sigma}}(x,y;\lambda)\right)=\frac{18\alpha}{\lambda^{2}}\delta_{ij}

(B.13)

∎

Appendix C
Stochastic Integration of random fields

The integral of a GRSF is defined as the limit of a Riemann sum of the field over the partition of a domain.

Proposition 3.1
.

Let
{\bm{\mathfrak{D}}}\subset{\mathbb{R}}^{n}
be a (closed) domain with boundary
\partial{\bm{\mathfrak{D}}}
and
x=(x_{1},...,x_{n})\subset{\bm{\mathfrak{D}}}
. Let
\bm{\mathfrak{D}}=\bigcup_{q=1}^{M}\bm{\mathfrak{D}}_{1}
be a partition of
\bm{\mathfrak{D}}
with
\bm{\mathfrak{D}}_{q}\bigcap\bm{\mathfrak{D}}_{q}=\varnothing
if
p\neq q
. Let
x^{(q)}\in\bm{\mathfrak{D}}_{q}
for all
q=1...M
. Note
x^{(q)}\equiv(x_{1}^{(q)},...x_{n}^{(q)})
. Then
x^{(1)}\in\bm{\mathfrak{D}}_{1},x^{(2)}\in\bm{\mathfrak{D}}_{2},...,x^{(M)}\in\bm{\mathfrak{D}}
. Let
\mathrm{Vol}(\bm{\mathfrak{D}}_{q})
be the volume of the partition
\bm{\mathfrak{D}}_{q}
so that
\mathrm{vol}(\bm{\mathfrak{D}})=\sum_{q}^{M}\mathrm{Vol}(\bm{\mathfrak{D}})
. Similarly, if
\partial\bm{\mathfrak{D}}
is the surface or boundary of

{\bm{\mathfrak{D}}}
then let

\bm{\mathfrak{D}}=\bigcup_{\xi=1}^{M}\partial\bm{\mathfrak{D}}{q}

(C.1)

be a partition of
\partial\bm{\mathfrak{D}}
with
\partial\bm{\mathfrak{D}}_{\xi}
be a partition of the boundary or surface into N constituents. Let
\bm{x}^{(q)}\in\partial\bm{\mathfrak{D}}_{q}
for all
q=1...M
. Note
x^{(q)}\equiv(x_{1}^{(q)},...x_{n}^{(q)})
. Then
x^{(1)}\in\partial\bm{\mathfrak{D}}_{1},x^{(2)}\in\partial\bm{\mathfrak{D}}_{2},...,x^{(H)}\in\partial\bm{\mathfrak{D}}
. Let
\|{\mathscr{W}}_{q}\|\equiv\mu(\partial\bm{\mathfrak{D}}_{q})
be the surface area of the partition
\partial\bm{\mathfrak{D}}_{q}
so that
\mu\bm{\mathfrak{D}}=\bigcap_{q=1}^{M}A(\partial\bm{\mathfrak{D}}_{q}
. The total volume and area of
\bm{\mathfrak{D}}
is

\displaystyle\mathrm{Vol}(\bm{\mathfrak{D}})=\sum_{q=1}^{M}(\bm{\mathfrak{D}}_{q})=\sum_{q=1}^{M}v(\bm{\mathfrak{D}}_{q})

(C.2)

\displaystyle\mathrm{Area}(\partial\bm{\mathfrak{D}})=\sum_{q=1}^{M}\mathrm{Area}(\partial\bm{\mathfrak{D}}_{q})=\sum_{q=1}^{M}\mathrm{Area}(\bm{\mathfrak{D}}_{q})

(C.3)

Given the probability triplet
(\Omega,{\psi},\mathbb{I\!P})
then a Gaussian random field on
\bm{\mathfrak{D}}
for all
x\in\bm{\mathfrak{D}}
is
{\mathscr{R}}:\omega\times\bm{\mathfrak{D}}\rightarrow{\mathbb{R}}
and
{\mathscr{R}}(x^{q},\omega)\in{\mathscr{W}}_{q}
exists for all
x^{(q)}\in\bm{\mathfrak{D}}_{q}
and
\omega\in\Omega
. The stochastic volume integral and the stochastic surface integral are

\displaystyle\int_{\bm{\mathfrak{D}}}{\mathscr{R}}(x;\omega)dU_{n}(x)=\lim_{all~v(\mathscr{W}_{q})\uparrow 0}\sum_{q=1}^{M}{\mathscr{R}}(x^{(q)};\omega)\mathrm{Vol}(\bm{\mathfrak{D}}_{q})

(C.4)

\displaystyle\int_{\partial\bm{\mathfrak{D}}}{\mathscr{R}}(x;\omega)dU_{n-1}(x)=\lim_{all~\mathrm{Area}(\partial\bm{\mathfrak{D}}_{q})\uparrow 0}\sum_{q=1}^{M}{\mathscr{R}}(x^{(q)};\omega)Area(\partial\bm{\mathfrak{D}}_{q})

(C.5)

When a Gaussian random field is integrated, it is the limit of a linear combination of Gaussian random variables/fields so it is again Gaussian.

Next, the stochastic expectations or averages are defined.

Proposition 3.2
.

Since

{\bm{\mathbb{I\!E}}}\bigg\langle\bullet\bigg\rangle=\int_{\bm{\mathfrak{D}}}(\bullet)d{\mathbb{I\!P}}(\omega)

(C.6)

the expectation of the volume integral is as follows.

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathbb{I\!E}}}\bigg\langle\int_{\mathbf{Q}}{\mathscr{R}}(x;\omega)dU_{n}(x)\bigg\rangle\equiv\int\!\!\int_{\bm{\mathfrak{D}}}{{\mathscr{R}}}(x;\omega)dmU_{n}(x)d\mathbb{I\!P}(\omega)

(C.7)

\displaystyle=\lim_{M\uparrow\infty}\lim_{all~\mathrm{Vol}(\bm{\mathfrak{D}}_{q})\uparrow 0}\int_{\Omega}\sum_{q=1}^{M}{{\mathscr{R}}}(x^{(q)};\omega)\mathrm{Vol}(\bm{\mathfrak{D}}_{q})d\bm{\mathbb{I\!P}}(\omega)=0

(C.8)

which vanishes for GRSFs since
\bm{\mathbb{I\!E}}\big\langle{{\mathscr{R}}}(x^{(q)})\big\rangle=0
. Similarly, for the stochastic surface integrals

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathbb{I\!E}}}\bigg[\int_{\bm{\mathfrak{D}}}{\mathscr{R}}(x;\omega)d^{n-1}x\bigg]=\lim_{all~area({{\mathscr{R}}}_{q})\uparrow 0}\mathbb{I\!E}\left\langle\sum_{q=1}^{M}{{\mathscr{R}}}(x^{(q)};\omega)A(\partial{{\mathscr{R}}}_{q})\right\rangle

(C.9)

or

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathbb{I\!E}}}\left\langle\int_{\partial{\bm{\mathfrak{D}}}}{\mathscr{R}}(x;\omega)dU_{n-1}(x)\right\rangle\equiv\int_{\Omega}\int_{\partial{\bm{\mathfrak{D}}}}{\mathscr{R}}(x;\omega)d^{n-1}xd\bm{\mathsf{I\!P}}(\omega)

(C.10)

\displaystyle=\lim_{all~\mu(\partial{\bm{\mathfrak{D}}}_{q})\uparrow 0}\int_{\Omega}\sum_{q=1}^{M}{\mathscr{R}}(x^{(q)};\omega)Area(\partial{\bm{\mathfrak{D}}}_{q})d{\mathbb{I\!P}}(\omega)=0

(C.11)

Given an integral (or summation) over a random field or stochastic process, the Fubini theorem states that the expectation of the integral or sum over a random field is equivalent to the integral or sum of the expectation of the field.

Theorem 3.3
.

Let
{\mathscr{R}}(x)
be a random field not necessarily Gaussian, existing for all
x\in\bm{\mathfrak{D}}
with expectation
\bm{\mathbb{I\!E}}\langle{\mathscr{R}}(x)\rangle
, not necessarily zero. Then

{\bm{\mathbb{I\!E}}}\bigg\langle\int_{\bm{\mathfrak{D}}}{{\mathscr{R}}}(x)d\mu(x)\bigg\rangle\equiv\int_{{\bm{\mathfrak{D}}}}{\bm{\mathbb{I\!E}}}\bigg\langle{\mathscr{R}}(x)\bigg\rangle dU_{n}(x)

(C.12)

For a set of N random fields
{{\mathscr{R}}}_{q}(x)

{\bm{\mathbb{I\!E}}}\bigg\langle\sum_{q=1}^{N}{{\mathscr{R}}}_{q}(x)\bigg\rangle=\sum_{q=1}^{N}\bm{\mathbb{I\!E}}\bigg\langle{{\mathscr{R}}}_{q}(x)\bigg\rangle

(C.13)

It is also possible to define a ’mollifier’ or convolution integral.

Proposition 3.4
.

Let
(x,y)\in{\bm{\mathfrak{D}}}
and let
\mathbf{K}(x-y)
be a smooth function of
(x,y)
that will depend on the separation
\|x-{y}\|
. Given the GRSF
{\mathscr{R}}(y)
define the volume and surface integral convolutions

\displaystyle{\mathscr{J}}(x)=\mathsf{\Sigma}(x-y){\mathscr{R}}(y)\equiv\int_{\bm{\mathfrak{D}}}\mathbf{K}(x-y){{\boxtimes}}{\mathscr{R}}(y)dU_{n}(y),~(x,y)\in\bm{\mathfrak{D}}

(C.14)

\displaystyle{\mathscr{J}}(x)={\mathsf{\Sigma}}(x-y){\mathscr{R}}(y)\equiv\int_{\bm{\mathfrak{D}}}\mathbf{K}(x-y){{\boxtimes}}{\mathscr{R}}(y)dU_{n-1}(y),(x,y)\in\partial{\bm{\mathfrak{D}}}

(C.15)

For example, if
\mathsf{\Sigma}(x-y)
is a Gaussian function then the random field
{\mathscr{R}}(x)
can be ’Gaussian smoothed’ on a scale
\ell
.

\displaystyle{\mathscr{J}}(x)={\mathsf{\Sigma}}(x-y){\mathscr{R}}(y)\equiv\mathsf{C}\int_{{\bm{\mathfrak{D}}}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{\ell^{2}}\right){{\boxtimes}}{\mathscr{R}}(y)dU_{n}(y),~(x,y)\in{\mathbf{I\!H}}

(C.16)

\displaystyle{\mathscr{J}}(x)={\mathsf{\Sigma}}(x-y){\mathscr{R}}(y)\equiv\mathsf{C}\int_{{\bm{\mathfrak{D}}}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{\ell^{2}}\right){{\boxtimes}}{\mathscr{R}}(y)dU_{n-1}(y),(x,y)\in\partial{\bm{\mathfrak{D}}}

(C.17)

For the heat kernel
h(x-y,t)
the convolution give a time-dependent GRSF that solves the heat equation
\square{\mathscr{R}}(x,t)=0
for random initial boundary data.

\displaystyle\mathscr{J}(x,t)=h(x-y,t)*{\mathscr{J}(y)}\equiv\int_{{\bm{\mathfrak{D}}}}\frac{1}{(4\pi t)^{n/2}}\exp\left(-\frac{\|x-y\|^{2}}{4t}\right)\big({\mathscr{J}(y)}dU_{n}(y),~(x,y)\in{\mathbf{Q}}

(C.18)

The result is extended to include GRSFs
{\mathscr{R}}(x)
.

Proposition 3.5
.

Let
{\mathscr{R}}(x)
be a regulated GRSF defined for all
x\in{\bm{\mathfrak{D}}}
such that
\bm{\mathbb{I\!E}}\langle{\mathscr{R}}(x)\rangle=0
and

\mathbb{I\!E}\bigg\langle\big|{\mathscr{R}}(x)\big|^{\ell}\bigg\rangle=\frac{1}{2}[\mathsf{C}^{\ell/2}+(-1)^{\ell}\mathsf{C}^{\ell/2}]

(C.19)

so that
\bm{\mathbb{I\!E}}\bigg\langle\big|{\mathscr{R}}(x)\big|^{\ell}\bigg\rangle=0
for all odd p. Then

\displaystyle{\bm{\mathbb{I\!E}}}\bigg\langle\bigg|\int_{{\bm{\mathfrak{D}}}}{\mathscr{R}}(x)d\mu(x)\bigg|^{\ell}\bigg\rangle

\displaystyle~\leq\frac{1}{2}[\mathsf{C}^{\ell/2}+(-1)^{\ell}\mathsf{C}^{\ell/2}]\bigg|\int_{{\bm{\mathfrak{D}}}}d\mu(x)\bigg|^{\ell}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}[\alpha^{\ell/2}+(-1)^{\ell}\alpha^{\ell/2}]\mathrm{Vol}[{\bm{\mathfrak{D}}}]^{\ell}

(C.20)
